1

5 / 8

方がリスクとなり、その能力を誰が保有し、誰が利用や移転を管理するのかが重要な論点とな
っている。

安全確保と開発競争のジレンマと協調を阻む構造 
これら二つの脅威は相互に影響している。AI の能力が高まるほど、安全対策の必要性と、そ
の能力を保有する経済的・安全保障上の価値がともに高まり、企業間・国家間の開発競争が激
しくなる。競争を優先すれば安全対策が後回しになりやすいが、単独で開発ペースを落とせば、
減速しない企業や国家が技術的な優位を得る可能性がある。このため、危険性を認識しても開
発競争から離脱しにくいというジレンマが生じる。 また、危険な能力へのアクセス制限や安全
規制は、悪用や拡散を抑える一方、能力の集中や利用格差 の拡大といった別のジレンマを生み
かねない（図表2）。 
こうした二つのジレンマへの対応には、企業や国家による協調が必要となる。ただし、AI で
は開発の主体と最先端の能力が国家ではなく民間企業に集中しているため、 協調は企業間と国
家間の二つの層で求められ、その難しさもそれぞれ異なる。 
企業間では、各社が安全対策の必要性を共有し、他社も同等の対策を実施すると確認できれ
ば、歩調を合わせる余地がある。相互の協力が期待できる状況では、協調が各社にとって最も
望ましく、抜け駆けする誘因は小さい。こうした安全対策の共通化は、協調の成否が相互の信
頼にかかるという点で、いわゆる「鹿狩りゲーム（全員が協力すれば最大の成果が得られるが、
他者の協力を信じられなければ各自が確実だが小さな成果を選んでしまう状況を表すゲーム理
論のモデル）」に近い構図である。実際、OpenAI、Anthropic、Google は、AI の安全性をめぐる
企業間協調について協議を進めている11。ただし、中国企業やオープンモデルとの競争も続い
ており、協調に加わらない主体が存在すれば、参加企業も 競争に備えざるを得ず、企業間協調
は不安定になる。 
国家間では、相手国が減速すれば抜け駆けして優位に立てる一方、相手国が開発を続ければ
減速した自国だけが後れを取る。このため、相手国の行動にかかわらず、自国は国内企業の開
発を後押しすることが合理的になりやすい。その結果、双方が減速する方が互いに望ましい場
合でも、双方が開発を続ける状態に陥りやすい。これは、いわゆる「囚人のジレンマ（各自が
自分にとって合理的な選択をした結果、全員にとって望ましくない結果に陥る状況を表すゲー
ム理論のモデル） 」に近い。合意によって減速を約束しても、各国には約束を破る誘因が残る
うえに、相手国が守っているかを確認することも難しい。国家間競争は自国企業に開発継続を
促すため、企業間の協調だけでは安全確保と開発競争のジレンマを解消できるとは限らない。

11 CNBC“OpenAI, Google, Anthropic discussing collaboration on AI safety issues”, September 15, 
2026.

図表 2 協調を阻む安全確保と開発競争のジレンマ

3. 企業間協調の可能性と限界、日本の役割 
安全性をめぐる企業間協調は機能するか 
OpenAI、Anthropic、Google DeepMind による協調は、安全対策を共通の競争条件とし、個社
だけが不利益を負う問題を緩和する可能性がある。ただし、協調の対象によって実現可能性は
異なる。能力評価の手法、第三者検証、インシデント報告などの情報共有や共通基準づくりは
比較的進めやすい。一方、開発ペースそのものの合意は競争を直接制約するため、抜け駆けの
誘因が残るほか、独占禁止法（反トラスト法）に抵触するおそれもある。実際、米国では、開
発減速をめぐる各社の呼びかけが違法な競争制限に当たるとして、消費者による集団訴訟が提
起されている12。 
前章で示した協調を阻む構造を踏まえると、企業間協調が機能するには、①主要企業の参加、
②検証の信頼性、③副作用の抑制という三つの条件が求められる。 
一つ目の主要企業の参加では、主要な開発主体が協調に参加し、不参加企業との競争に対処
できることが求められる。不参加主体が開発を続ければ、参加企業にも安全策を緩める誘因が
生じるため、協調の参加範囲を広げるとともに、不参加主体との競争によって合意が崩れない
仕組みが必要となる。 
二つ目の検証の信頼性では、合意した安全策の実施状況を相互に確認できることが求められ
る。他社が合意を守っていると確認できなければ、自社だけが不利益を負うことへの警戒から、
協調は維持されにくい。ただし、最先端モデルを評価できる主体は限られるほか、評価期間の

短縮や、評価中のモデルが評価の意図を認識して行動を変える可能性など、検証の信頼性を損
なう要因もある。 
三つ目の副作用の抑制では、協調が市場集中や競争制限につながることを防ぐ必要がある。
前章でみたように、高度で費用のかかる安全基準は既存大手への集中を強めかねない。また、
政府や評価機関が主要企業の知見や資源に依存すれば、企業の利益が過度に制度に反映される
おそれもある。このため、基準形成の透明性や外部主体の関与を確保し、安全性を理由とする
協調が既存企業の地位を守り、市場競争を制限する手段とならないようにする必要がある。 
企業間の自主的な合意だけでは、不参加企業に網をかけられず、検証の独立性や基準づくり
の透明性も確保しにくい。このため、三つの条件を満たしていくには、企業間で形成された安
全基準や評価方法を各国の制度に取り込み、国際的な枠組みを通じて共有する必要がある 。国
家間で開発減速に合意するなど、開発ペースそのものを調整することは難しくとも、能力評価
やインシデント報告の基準を共有し、各国が共通の基準に基づいて企業の対応を検証する仕組
みは構築し得る。 
こうした仕組みの実効性を確保するには、企業間で形成された基準を国際的に共有するだけ
でなく、各国の言語や利用環境において安全策が機能するかを確認し、その実施状況を独立し
た立場から検証できる主体を増やす必要がある。

日本は国際的な枠組みづくりで存在感を示せるか 
最先端 AI の開発を米国企業などが先導する中でも、日本が AI の安全性をめぐる国際的な枠
組みの形成に関与する余地は残されている。日本は、2023 年 5 月の G7 広島サミットを契機に
立ち上げられた広島 AI プロセスを通じて、高度な AI を開発する組織に求める国際指針や行動
規範の形成を主導してきた13。広島 AI プロセスでは、国際行動規範への対応状況を各組織が自
主的に報告・公表する「報告枠組み」も運用されており、各社のリスク管理や安全対策を国際
的に可視化・比較するための基盤が形成されている 14。ただし、企業間で形成された安全基準
を各国の制度へ接続する段階では、こうした原則や枠組みを提唱した実績だけで、日本が引き
続き国際的な枠組み形成に実質的に関与できるとは限らない。 
前節で整理したとおり、企業間で形成された安全基準や評価方法を国際的な枠組みへ発展さ
せるには、各国の言語や利用環境における実効性を 独立した立場から検証できる主体を増やす
必要がある。評価・検証に必要なのは、評価対象モデルへのアクセス、評価手法や評価環境、
専門人材であり、自国で最先端AI を開発する能力を有することは、必ずしも前提とならない。
日本が国際的な枠組みの形成に実質的に関与するには、まずは最先端AI の能力・リスクに関す
る評価結果や、安全対策の実効性を自ら技術的に検証できる体制を整えることが重要となる。 
日本には、その足掛かりとなる実績や知見がある。日本政府が 2024 年に設立した、AI の安

13 広島 AI プロセス「広島AI プロセスについて」（2026 年10 月5 日最終閲覧） 
14 広島 AI プロセス「報告枠組み」（2026 年10 月5 日最終閲覧）

8 / 8
全性に関する評価や基準策定を担う機関、AI セーフティ・インスティテュート（AISI）15は、
10 か国が参加するAI モデルの国際共同テスト演習に加わり、複数の言語におけるLLM（大規模
言語モデル）の評価手法や AI エージェントの評価手法の改善に取り組んできた16。日本が知見
を蓄積しつつある領域である。
このように、日本には評価・検証の足掛かりとなる実績があるものの、それを本格的に担う
ための体制整備はこれからである。AISI はこれまで、評価基準やガイドといった「物差しづく
り」を重視してきた。2025 年の年次レポートでは、今後の方向性として「自ら評価する能力を
もつ」ことと「国際協調を主導する」ことを掲げている17。日本政府も 2026 年 7 月に改定・閣
議決定した「人工知能基本計画」において、AISI の抜本的強化を引き続き掲げている。同年 6
月には、将来的に AISI が最先端 AI などを評価できる体制の構築に向け、必要な手法や評価環
境を検討する調査が開始された18。

日本に期待されるのは、企業間で形成された安全基準をそのまま受け入れるのではなく、各
国の言語や利用環境における実効性を検証し、国際的な枠組みへ接続する際の橋渡し役である。
そのためには、AISI の専門人材や評価環境の充実に加え、公開前を含む評価対象モデルへのア
クセス、機微情報を扱うための情報管理体制、他国のAI 安全機関との共同評価体制を整える必
要がある。AI の能力向上と企業間協調をめぐる議論が急速に進む中、最先端 AI を自ら評価す
る日本の体制づくりはなお調査・検討段階にある。このままでは、最先端 AI の開発だけでな
く、その安全性をめぐる国際的な枠組みの形成でも後れを取りかねない。広島AI プロセスで培
った実績を活かし、国際的な枠組みづくりに継続的に関与していくためにも、最先端 AI の能
力・リスクに関する評価結果や安全対策の実効性を、自ら技術的に検証できる体制を早急に整
えることが求められる。

Source PDF: all report pdf/Allianz_2026-10-20_European defense one year on_ Money is not the problem, capacity (and delivery) are_1d7796ef.pdf
Report source: Allianz Trade
Date: 2026-10-20

### Page 1

1

In Summary 
• European defense one year on: bold commitments, but is delivery keeping pace? One year 
into Europe's landmark rearmament announcements: defense spending has increased, 
financial commitments have expanded (e.g. SAFE loans, +60% commitments in 2028-2034 EU 
draft budget ) and order books have reached record highs . But industrial investment and 
delivery have yet to catch up. Defense spending growth has been cut in half  (9% vs. above 
20% in 2024/2025) , and is  set to reach  2.6% of GDP by 2030  (3.1% of GDP by 2035 ), falling 
short of the EU 2030 target of 3% and the 2035 NATO target of 3.5% of GDP . This means a 
funding gap of EUR255bn and EUR575bn, respectively.  
• In equity markets, defense companies are facing a painful reality check but the hype is not 
over yet. European defense has lost its magic (+5% YTD but -8% maximum drawdown) after 
three consecutive years of strong performance (35 -60%), a clear reality check from investors 
as countries take diverging paths : Baltic  and Nordic countries, as well as  Germany and 
Poland are leading the effort while Italy and Spain are not using the available fiscal tools to 
boost spending. Capital has been mostly channeled into  equipment, rather than long-term 
infrastructure project s (0.11% of GDP  against NATO recommendations of 1.5% of GDP). In 
term of innovation, defense is lagging the AI and robotics conversion but rising interest from 
the private investment market (above EUR10bn vs. EUR2.6bn in 202 5) suggests that the 
innovation cycle still has strong funding leeway. 
• Money is not the constraint , conversion is. Governments have poured commitments into a 
sector where newcomers cannot quickly acquire what incumbents built over decades, so 
excess demand turns into production bottlenecks and postponed projects rather than output. 
Backlogs stand at more than five times revenue while the capex ratio has stalled near 5% . 
Diverging national interests , no supranational steer  and the absence of conditionality 
mandatory clauses in public financial support give the industry little reason to invest at a time 
when higher interest rates and material costs from the Iran conflict have cut m argins (from 
10% to 8%). The shortage of skilled labor adds to the pressure as firms compete for engineers 
with civilian sectors that pay more, and cannot quickly transition their workforce into new 
complex programs. The shortage is sharpest where Europe started late : drones, space and 
cybersecurity segments account for 26%, 16% and 13% of open defense vacancies. 
• The ongoing depletion of US interceptor stocks is another warning for Europe: external  
dependence is both a security risk and a capital -efficiency problem.  To fill urgent gaps, 
Europe buys from abroad, and the import ratio has risen from 20% in 2021 to 45% in 2025. 
The US’s share in European imports has fallen from 71% to 48% but it still supplies almost half, 
and aircraft and missiles make up 82% of US purchases, with few alternatives for air defense 
and long-range missiles. We estimate it could take up to five years to rebuild some US stocks; 
until then, US domestic demand will come first, and Europe will wait in the queue behind it. 
Dependence is also expensive: Missile import prices are up 116% on the 2019 -2021 average, 
so each euro buys less capacity than budgets assume.  
• The way forward: Two horizons but one single industrial roadmap. In the short term, Europe 
needs to close the gaps in long -range missiles, air defense and cyber, and redirect idle 
automotive capacity to defense. Furthermore, it needs to back this with grants for 
infrastructure (a 0.5-1% of GDP corridor, or EUR760bn to E UR1.4trn cumulative to 2035) and 
fiscal flexibility conditional on results. In the long term, Europe needs to build supranational 
reserves and invest in drones, space, AI, robotics and quantum. These would spread benefits 
across the economy and protect the export model. Success should be judged on new capacity, 
spillovers, modernization and reliance rate, not on the share of GDP alone.  
Guillaume Dejean 
Senior Sector advisor

trade.com

Ano Kuhanathan 
Head of Corporate Research

Allianz Research | 20 October 2026 
European defense one year on: Money is not the 
problem, capacity (and delivery) are

### Page 2

Rebuilding European defense: ambition meets reality 
One year after the Rearm Europe roadmap was unveiled, the question is no longer one of intent but means. Does 
Europe have the financial and material resources to match its rhetoric?  European defense spending is rising, but 
not fast enough. At an expected 2.3% of GDP in 2026, up from 2.2% in 2025, it is set to record a second consecutive 
year above the 2% threshold. Yet the headline masks a sharp deceleration: Annual growth in outlays is falling to 
about 9%, from more than 20% in 2024-2025, and will drift toward 4-7% by 2028-2030. This new 5-10% regime looks 
insufficient for to meet the ambitions of Rearm Europe. Based on our growth and inflation forecasts for 2026 -2027, 
and a moderate 1% growth/2% inflation path thereafter, spending is likely to climb  to only 2.6% of GDP by 2030, 
missing the 3% target. The shortfall is material: a cumulative EUR255bn funding gap over 2026 -2030, widening to 
EUR575bn if we consider NATO’s new 3.5% of GDP target for core military spending. 
Figures 1 & 2: European defense spending trajectory by 2030  / cumulative spending gap based on 3% and 3.5% 
GDP budget target (in EUR bn)

Sources: 
 European Defense Agency (EDA),  
Allianz Research  
Europe's rearmament challenge is increasingly about execution, not just ambition. At the current pace, doubling 
Europe's 2025 military equipment stock would take around five years, leaving little room for delays before the 2030 
deadline. Faster progress would require not only higher defense spending, but also a greater share of budgets 
devoted to equipment and infrastructure, alongside a stronger European industrial base. Redirecting procurement 
towards European manufacturers  by reducing the share of imports from 45% to 25% could help accelerate capacity 
expansion. Under the most optimi stic scenario, these combined measures could shorten the timeline from five to 
around three years. But Europe cannot supply itself and remains highly dependent on foreign partners to procure 
its military equipment. The defense spending allocation shows a 35-40% disbursement into new equipment (vs. 30-
35% on NATO standards)  and 0-5% into infrastructure construction/maintenance, which is quite strong by NATO 
standards but not as ambitious as promised.

Money is no longer the main constraint: capacity is 
Money is not Europe’s binding constraint . The draft 2028 -2034 EU budget lifts defense-related commitments to 
around EUR270bn, at least 60% above the roughly EUR170bn of 2021 -2027. That estimate excludes the European 
Investment Bank, which devoted 4 -5% of its annual envelope to defense in 2025 -2026, or EUR4 -5bn a year. 
Extrapolated over seven years, this adds EUR30-35bn and takes the total mark-up to about 80%. The signal matters 
as much as the amount. While national budgets face fiscal pressure, Brussels is locking in a multi-year commitment, 
with no sign of fading effort at the European level. The composition is shifting too: military funds, marginal in 2021 -
2027, become the largest block of the next envelope, a clear break with past practice. Off -budget instruments 
reinforce the message, and new tools modelled on current program s could follow if demand and delivery justify 
them. For industrials and investors, the financing architecture is in place and still expanding. The question is no 
longer whether capital exists, but whether it reaches the factories that must turn it into equ ipment.

### Page 4

SAFE loans illustrate the solution but also the problem. Off-budget and capped at EUR150bn, they should channel 
roughly EUR80bn into regional efforts if distribution is gradual and homogeneous. The reality is far from that. Only 
EUR12bn has been disbursed, about 8% of the total, against EUR131bn formally adopted. Poland, the largest 
beneficiary at EUR43.7bn, has drawn 15% of its envelope. France, Italy, Belgium and Spain have received nothing 
yet, and  Hungary’s EUR16.2bn is still pending. Industrials have long blamed red tape and lengthy validation 
procedures for construction delays and underperforming projects, and SAFE is repeating the pattern. The 
multiplicity of programs (EDIP, FED, AGILE, innovati on funds, EIB defense lines) compounds the issue: they do not 
connect to industrial segments and leave firms navigating parallel application processes. Participation is uneven as 
well, with several states slow to apply. Without faster execution, the money risks arriving after the equipment gap 
has widened, and after the political window that justified it has narrowed.  
Table 1: Safe procurement program allocation and utilization rate

Sources: 
 European Commission,  
Allianz Research

The European fiscal impulse tool was not used to its full potential. Europe’s fiscal loosening  leeway, authorizing 
18 members to run until extra deficits up to 1.5% of GDP for defense spending showed some results, but not as much 
as expected. Among positive results, it clearly helped push military spending back above NATO’s former 2%-of-GDP 
benchmark in 2025 for the first time , and keep the momentum up in  2026. Among the 18 eligible countries, 12 are 
expected to run deficits above 3% in 2026 ( 70%) and only eight of them recorded a double -digit growth of military 
spending over the 2025/2026 period compared with 2024 ahead of the launch of the tool . If some countries like 
Poland, Latvia, Estonia or even Germany are good examples of the positive externalities of the NEC on European 
defense ramp-up efforts, the overall picture is quite uneven at the European level. Indeed, a higher deficit is not the 
guarantee of stronger defense spending, as showed by  Belgium, Hungary and Slovakia , which carry high deficits 
but grew military budgets by only 4-5% in 2026. Italy, Spain and the Netherlands raised spending by a modest 5-8% 
and stayed close to Brussels’ 3% line. With interest rates high and markets volatile, France’s deficit being the 
cautionary example, that prudence is understandable. The NEC works, but it is still far from being used to its full 
potential. This clearly demonstrates that Europe is not running out of resources to rebuild its army but rather of will, 
at public or private level.

Procurement is also crowding out the long term . Spending favors short-term equipment gaps, while defense 
infrastructure stands at only 0.11% of GDP in 2026, a small share of regional investment. Quick buys beat durable 
capacity, which leaves the underlying production base no stronger. The undersubscription to SAFE loans is the 
sharpest evidence: the program did not attract enough demand, and EUR10 -20bn is likely to be reallocated to 
Ukraine. Europeans are not short of funding but of capacity to buy. Sourcing rules limit purchases outside Europe, 
yet domestic supply cannot absorb the orders either, so governments can neither buy abroad nor at home as much 
as they would like. Strict rules on foreign investment in sensitive defense assets further limit outside capital for new 
capacity, closing another route to faster expansion. The pattern points one way: Europe’s bottleneck is industrial, 
not financial. The next euro of ambition should go to factories, infrastructure and faster exe cution, not to larger 
envelopes. Otherwise Europe will keep announcing money it cannot spend.

Europe’s industrial conundrum: a record-high backlog did not translate into new investment. European defense 
has never had so much demand, yet its largest firms are not building the capacity to serve it. Order backlogs have 
hit record highs, at more than five times trailing revenue for the ten biggest listed groups, driven mostly by national 
governments. Revenue still expands at around +10% on a 12-month trailing basis, but the trend is flattening, which 
raises a question: has the peak already been reached? Capex points the same way. After the surge of 2023 -2024, 
the capex ratio has stabilized around 5%, a natural ceiling that matches the  lack of new impulse on sales. Orders 
are being booked faster than they can be converted into output. This is the industrial conundrum. The timing for 
investing could hardly be better, with full order books and political tailwinds. But two brakes are holding firms back: 
a monetization lag between orders and cash, and mild confidence in the durability of state commitment. Managers 
would rather move slowly than be left with overcapacity if budgets disappoint. The consequence is a self -inflicted 
bottleneck: the demand is there, but the supply response is rationed by caution.  
Figure 8 & 9: Revenue & investment trend of top 10 biggest defense firms in Europe* / Ammunition infrastructure

The d efense industry is adopting a defensive approach to cash management: securing margin s rather than 
chasing new revenue . Scaling up fast without disrupting current activity or inflating costs is harder than early 
optimism suggested, and the initial enthusiasm for capacity expansion has given way to caution as delivery under 
high-stakes pressure turns out to be tougher than p lanned. Visibility is the second brake. Future budgets and 
governance remain uncertain in a shifting political landscape, with the far -right push visible in several European 
countries adding doubt over the orientation and durability of defen se priorities. All these elements put together 
drive a weak incentive to invest aggressively  in expensive and long -term project s. Market conditions have also 
turned. Tensions in the Middle East have lifted energy and raw material costs, and the S&P GSCI industrial metals 
index is back near its 2022 highs, while higher borrowing costs make every risk more expensive. The margin picture  
shows the squeeze: net profit margins of the top European defen se firms peaked around 10% in 2025 and have 
slipped back to about 8%. In this environment, protecting margins is more rational than chasing new revenue. 
Backlogs are already full and the sector enjoys relative protection from foreign competition, so there is little need 
to take risks to win new orders. Defen se groups are therefore becoming defensive themselves, prioriti zing cash 
discipline over expansion. The outcome is an industry that has the orders but not the appetite, and a Europe whose 
rearmament will be paced by corporate prudence rather than by political ambition.

The undesirable effects of a backlog. Governments have poured commitments into a sector where newcomers 
cannot acquire in a few months what incumbents built over decades, so excess demand turns into production 
bottlenecks and postponed projects rather than output. The structure compounds the problem. Defense ministries 
have become the primary customer, competition is weak and contracts are concentrated on a handful of players. 
That is the captive market trap: a secured backlog, instead of lifting investment appetite, breeds complacency and 
decision inertia on new infrastructure. Rheinmetall is the case in point. With about 60% of its backlog coming from 
the Bundeswehr, it cut its revenue guidance in Q2 after removing a naval project it had committed to but could not 
deliver as it was far from its core ammunition business. Diversification is sound only when it is coherent and gradual. 
Markets have been unforgiving: the share has lost about 35% year to date, and market capitali zation fall en to 
roughly EUR 40 -45bn while forward EBIT growth has slipped from its 2026 peak of over 50% to below 50%. The 
sector that was once in the spotlight is now paying for the hype, with disappointing delivery feeding a market verdict 
that could weigh on the next round of investment.

Europe’s defense industry is running out of people before it runs out of orders.  We estimate the sector employs 
650,000-700,000 people, up 30-40% since 2021, while military spending has risen 75% and backlogs have more than 
doubled. The gap is widening. In our sample of leading firms, backlog growth per point of workforce growth was 
about 2 before 2025, rose to 3 in 2025 and reache d 4 in H1 2026, as EU rearmament program s and fiscal escape 
clauses poured money into order books. This is more than a capacity limit. Firms have little room to hire at scale: 
they compete for engineers with civilian sectors that pay more, and newcomers need years of training before they 
can deliver on complex program s. Expertise cannot be bought, only built, and in defense industrial lead times are 
long and some skills exist nowhere else. The shortage is sharpest where Europe started late. Drones and UAVs 
account for 26% of open defense vacancies, space 16%, intelligence and surveillance 13%, electronics 9%: segments 
in which Europe was largely absent until recently. Industry also risks overestimating what it can absorb. Money 
moves at political speed, learning curves at industrial speed. Scaling up with too few or poorly matched staff brings 
delays and failures, so the backlog will convert slowly.  
Figures 13 & 14: backlog value vs. workforce growth for top European defense firms (2024 -H1 2026) /  Current 
open jobs in Defense sectors in Europe, per activity segment breakdown

 LSEG Datastream,  
DefenseJobsEU
 .com
, 
Allianz Research  
Who is piloting the plane ? 
Europe’s defense effort is moving at two speeds. The 2025 -2026 comparison splits the continent into leaders 
(Lithuania, Latvia, Estonia and Poland ), which are heading toward defense spending at 4.5-5.5% of GDP in 2026 
and a pack of laggards, including France, Italy, Belgium, Czechia and Slovenia, which are still below 2.5%. Denmark 
and Sweden sit in between, above the diagonal. Geography sets the gradient first: Proximity to Russia determines 
the sense of emergency. But national equipment levels and domestic industrial strength matter as well. Frontline 
states are rebuilding from thin inventories, while larger Western economies feel less pressed and can lean on 
existing stocks and industrial bases. Politics reinforces the divide. Household surveys show strong appetite for 
intervention in Sweden, Estonia, Denmark, Poland and the UK, but little in France, Germany and Italy, where 
respondents also reject trading off ot her public spending for defense. Where popular support is thin or social 
cohesion fragile, governments hesitate to push ambition. The risk is that the largest economies, which hold the 
biggest industrial bases, set the slowest pace, leaving aggregate momentum dependent on a handful of s mall, 
frontline budgets. Europe cannot be defended by its Baltic fringe alone.

Europe’s biggest competitor is itself. It is quite straightforward to see that European nations do not ride in the same 
direction. A quick look at the  industrial project pipeline shows duplication rather than scale. Two rival next -
generation fighters are under way: the Global Combat Air Program ( GCAP) led by the trident UK, Italy, Japan and 
the French-led Air Combat Fighter (ACF), the successor to former French-German Future Combat Air System (FCAS) 
initiative whose failure this year  illustrates how  national interests can sink a supra -regional target. On land, four 
separate platforms (MGCS, Challenger 3, K2PL, A2CS) are built by four different coalitions. All are not European-
led as K2PL is a hybrid partnership between Poland and South Korea to replicate the flagship K2 tank used by the 
South Korean army . This sends a diverging signal : Europe can find partners other than the US for military 
procurement, but also it still needs foreign expertise to develop heavy equipment quickly  (three-to-five year 
timeline). For comparison, some joint projects, like the French-German Main Ground Combat System (MGCS), have 
an industrial lead of over 20 years, and the latter also faces industrial divergences between partners.  Behind what 
is said about the slow development of  FCAS and MGCS is a Franco-German leadership contest that is the cause of 
all this fragmentation. While two pilots are better than none, they do not share the same view in term s of defense: 
Paris pushes sovereignty and autonomous industrial capacity, while Berlin prioriti zes rapid re -equipment to be

effective if war comes. The current air defense pipeline shows the split. The German -led Sky Shield (ESSI) targets 
2027-2030, while the European Twister interceptor, driven by France, arrives in 2035 -2040. Both logics are 
defensible, but run in parallel they fragment demand, shorten production runs and raise uni t costs. The answer is 
not black or white. Europe needs short-term equipment to be credible, and buying off the shelf abroad is sometimes 
the fastest route, as K2PL with South Korea illustrates. But overreliance on external providers has a price: a weaker 
domestic industry, lost competitiveness and strategic dependence for decades. The workable middle path is to buy 
abroad for urgent gaps while anchoring long -cycle programs in genuine cooperation, with common requirements, 
shared leadership and pooled orders. Without that, Europe will spend more and get less.  
Industrial direction won’t come from a single voice but three. France, Germany and Italy account for about 80% of 
the European revenue among the world’s top 100 arms sellers, and for 67% of the EU’s military -related output in 
2026. The two measures point to different leaders. France dominates on corporate sales (37%), while Germany leads 
on production on its territory (34%). No major project can be defined, financed or delivered without all three, which 
makes their alignment the main lever for an investment trend. The setbacks on FCAS and MGCS did more than 
delay equipment: they fed doubts about whether Europe can ramp up production to meet its targets, and markets 
and managers read those signals closely. The trio should settle priorities, share leadership by project and commit 
to joint program with fixed schedules an d pooled funding. A credible Franco -German understanding would carry 
the most weight. It would show that cohesion is possible and give industry the visibility it needs to invest. Without it, 
the efforts of smaller countries will stay fragmented, whatever their budgets.

Beyond the rivalries, Europe’s military priori ty is  clearly the sky . Long -range missiles, air and missile defen se, 
drones and modernized fighters all answer the same shift in the nature of war. Modern conflict is moving away from 
massed land and naval engagements toward air strikes and drones aimed at tactical infrastructure, where 
disruption hurts the economy as much as the army. Ukraine made the point: Drones have become full combat tools, 
used for reconnaissance and targeting as well as for dropping munitions, at a fraction of the cost of traditional 
systems. The conflict involving Iran confirmed it, with data centers and power systems among the damaged assets. 
The implication is that deterrence now depends on striking deep, intercepting cheaply and protecting critical 
networks, rather than on heavy armor or large fleets. Europe’s list of priorities reflects that lesson. The consensus on 
what to buy is real, the remaining challenge is how to organize and fund it together.

Europe needs to bet on itself, not only for sovereign reasons 
External reliance has not disappeared but is changing shape. Foreign dependence has almost doubled since 
2022, from 24% to 45% of procurement, and the US share has dropped from 71% to 48%. But Washington is still the 
main supplier, and in the UK, the Nordics and the Baltics , it still supplies the large majority of external purchases. 
What is new is that buying abroad is starting to give way to partnering. German and Israeli primes already 
cooperate on missiles, loitering munitions and satellites, and a deal to turn a German car plant into an air defense 
site is taking shape. These are early signals, but they matter. Openness is the fastest way to meet short-term demand 
when domestic capacity is stretched , and real investment commitments on European soil turn a purchase into 
capacity. But openness is also hard to square with security stakes and sovereign risk: whoever owns the technology 
and the supply chain holds a lever over Europe’s defense. The condition is close monitoring: governance, ownership, 
access to critical know-how and exit options all need to be locked in upfront. This will not be a replicable template

### Page 12

12

for the whole sector but rather a tool for targeted, specific needs where Europe cannot wait for its own industry to 
catch up. 
Figures 19 & 20: Imports of arms, non-EU share & US share among non-EU imports / US arms import share in 
Europe, per region

Data as of 2025. Sources: SIPRI, Allianz Research  
Foreign dependence is also a price problem: inefficient capital allocation due to inflationary import costs.  Since 
2022, as Europe turned to non-European suppliers for arms and ammunition, import prices per kilogram have risen 
well beyond what a technology premium can justify. Compared with the 2019-2021 average, missiles now cost 116% 
more, more than double the pre-war level. Tanks and armored vehicles are up 83%, fighters 33% and helicopters 
18%. The pattern follows dependence. Where Europe has little or no domestic alternative, as with US interceptors 
and long-range missiles, suppliers have had room to pass premium margins on to buyers facing scarcity and urgent 
demand. Higher costs linked to AI expansion and the Middle East conflict play a role, but they do not explain the 
whole picture: prices have flattened this year for most categories despite renewed energy -driven inflation, which 
points to pricing power as much as input costs. The consequence is direct. Europe pays more for the same equipment, 
not because it is worth it but because it cannot wait. Each euro committed buys less capacity than budgets assume, 
so the real size of the rearmament is smaller than the headline.

### Page 13

The US ammunition crisis: an unpleasant but welcome wake -up call. The depletion of US missile stocks is a 
warning for Europe: a single supplier can fail it at any moment, by choice or by necessity. The Iran conflict has 
consumed interceptors at a pace production cannot match. Compared with pre -war levels, our reading of the data 
shows H1 2026 inventories down about 65% for Patriot, 45% for THAAD and 30% for Tomahawk. Washington’s 
answer is a rebuild: its FY27 request targets about 3,200 Patriot interceptors and 860 THAAD interceptors. But 
factories are the bottleneck, not budgets. Backlogs at US missile makers are growing at 28% a year against revenue 
growth of 9%, and orders now stand at about three times annual sales. On current ramp-up rates, we estimate four 
years to deliver the Patriot request and five for THAAD. Until then, US domestic demand will come first, and Europe 
will wait in the queue behind it. The US is not a last -resort partner, because it has no spare capacity to lend. The 
same weapons are now in short supply on both sides of the Atlantic, so Europe cannot count on  American stocks to 
cover its own air defense.

Europe’s defense industry needs three leaders, not one 
Rebuilding defense means thinking short-term with a long-term horizon state of mind. When defining European 
priorities in terms of procurement but also industrial investment, reducing US exports of arms and ammunitions to 
Europe would help to connect the dots . Aircraft account for 63% of EU imports from the US and missiles 19%, so 
these two segments make up over four -fifths of the total. Self -reliance has to be built there first. In the short term, 
Europe should widen its sourcing to cushion the post -Iran arbitrage in US production. On missiles, the options are 
narrow: Israel is the only credible supplier outside the US, since China and Russia are not an option. That choice 
should come with local production and investment commitments. On fighters, Europe can le an on its own 
technology and speed up upgrades, which makes cutting reliance on the US realistic even in the near term. On 
drones, Ukraine offers scale and unmatched combat experience, and Europe should plug into it through joint 
production and shared prac tice. Türkiye can support local manufacturing, and South Korea can help on armored 
vehicles and robotics as well.

Defense capacity should not be exclusively assessed in hardware . Software, IT and telecoms are overlooked, 
though new equipment carries a heavy digital load and produces vast amounts of data. Used well, that data is a 
performance driver. Used poorly, it is a vulnerability. Cybersecurity is the other missing layer: mod ern conflict is 
fought on networks as much as on the ground. It is no accident that, in the extra funding requested by the US after 
the Iran clash, cybersecurity stands at about 8% and is the largest dedicated capability line after munitions. Europe 
should match that signal. There is also a risk of new dependence on the US here. Leading AI models are ahead in 
detecting system flaws and patching them, and relying on them would hand a foreign provider a view into critical 
European systems. Europe should invest in open-weight models it can run, audit and adapt on its own infrastructure, 
and pair them with a European cyber shield. This is an under -funded segment and a strategic one: capacity that 
cannot be switched off from abroad.

A two-horizon roadmap 
Horizon 1 - Filling the short-term hole to secure protection minimum 
The most important action is being consistent. On the current path, spending reaches about 3.1% of GDP by 2035, 
close to EUR800bn a year. For a region that was below 2% only a few years ago, that is a strong result. The 3% 
objective may have been set too early, at a time when industry could not absorb the funds. The 3.5% and 5% paths 
show the stakes: they would require roughly EUR900bn and EUR1.3trn a year by 2035, volumes that factories and 
supply chains could not deliver on today’s footing. Policy should therefore be judged on more than one number. 
Four measures matter more. First, new output capacity: how many more missiles, vehicles and munitions Europe 
can produce each year. Second, industrial externalities for automotive, electronics, software and materials. Third, 
modernization: whether equipment and technology are up to date and are competitive for export market. Fourth, 
the reliance rate: the share of capability that depends on non -European suppliers. A budget that rises while these 
four indicators stagnate is a failure. A smaller one that raises  capacity and lowers dependence is a success. Europe 
should publish them alongside the spending ratio. 
Figure 27: EU-27 defense spending as % of GDP trajectory over 2026-2035 period

Sources: European Commission,  
European Defense Agency,  
Allianz Research  
Build the ground before the factories. Europe has bought the missiles and forgotten the ground they stand on. 
Infrastructure is where rearmament stalls: production sites, depots and logistics do not build themselves, and a 
sleepy private investment cycle will not wake without a push. Government s should provide it, through grants and 
subsidies aimed at defense infrastructure. NATO’s 1.5% of GDP line by 2035 should be read as a compass, not a 
deadline. Reaching it would mean covering a hole above EUR2trn, on top of procurement bills that are alrea dy 
heavy. Europe should set a realistic corridor of 0.5-1% instead, which still demands EUR760bn to EUR1.4trn of effort. 
Public money cannot replace the market, and it should not try. Its job is narrower: lower the entry cost, signal 
permanence and rekindle an investment engine slowed by high rates. Every euro of support should buy a 
commitment in return, on capacity, deadlines and European location. Defense becomes a new vertical of European 
industrial policy, planned with the ambition once reserved for energy and chips.

No free lunch: look at home before buying abroad . Europe’s car plants run below potential while its defen se lines 
run beyond it. This is an opportunity for European governments to play matchmaker and build up industrial bridges, 
especially as the automotive industry slows down . S teering idle automotive capacity, workforce and supplier 
networks toward defense primes in a first step, then lifting output without burning scarce capital would kill two birds 
with one stone: loosening  bottlenecks in defen se while easing the production cost structure for automakers . Each 
new link proves the two industrial agendas can be synchronized. Procurement must follow the same logic. Domestic 
orders come with capex commitments attached. Foreign technology is welcome only when the need is critical and 
recurrent, and only if it brings production and investment onto European soil. Buying abroad with no local return is 
a bill, not a strategy. The short -term priorities are narrow and clear: long -range missiles, air defen se to shield 
infrastructure and cyber tools against disinformation and external attacks. Europe should treat price as a security 
variable: build competing sources, pool orders to gain bargaining power and tie large contracts to production on 
European soil.

Flexibility is earned, not granted. The fiscal impulse is fading while rates are getting higher and stagflation risks in 
Europe are rising, which means public effort could weaken just when momentum is needed. Greater fiscal flexibility 
is required but  an extension of the national escape clause (NEC) is perhaps not the right answer to underpin a 
constant and robust investment cycle as results so far have been quite mixed. Incentives are missing and here is the 
crux of the capacity conundrum in Europe. Instead, a double approach in which a big military spender – either the 
state or a corporate - would be rewarded by a fiscal loosening measure and easier credit access  but support would 
be this time conditional and monitored against tangible results. Spending above budget lines would be authorized 
if, and only if, it is channeled into European-based industrial projects for identified critical equipment or resources , 
and key milestones are met on time. For firms, a tax-rebate of 1 to 2% could be applied in case of strong investment 
commitment that would be evaluate in function of their current capex polic ies and backlog level s. This should 
reassure sceptics who see another loosening of the rules as a loophole, and give markets what they want to see: 
ambition with discipline. Used well, a conditional waiver turns fiscal rules from an obstacle into an instrument of 
industrial strategy. Used badly, it becomes Europe’s next debt debate.

Horizon 2: Thinking on a 2030 defensive line 
Building supranational reserves as the ultimate disruption-mitigation tool. Let’s take US as example. Having the 
biggest and most performing defense industry does not prevent it from building up strategic reserves to anticipate 
market shocks and/or a wartime-led demand surge. Europe should do the same for critical equipment, arms and 
ammunition. A reserve cuts dependence on foreign supply and buys time when conflict closes borders and ports. It 
also needs ownership beyond national flags. Build supranational governance a nd a shared financing base, with 
European borrowing on the table, so that every capital feels concerned. Condition ing financial support on joint 
procurement channels  would end the national conflicts of interest that have sunk projects before. The ideal is a  
nation-free fleet of arms and ammunition, usable only under NATO governance , with clear intermediate targets so 
that private firms can plan capacity, hire and invest with confidence.

Modernize or be dependent again... Modernization is the best protection against new dependence, and it protects 
what Europe sells best: defense exports are a major revenue line, and they erode the moment the continent slips off 
the technology curve. The priorities are clear. First, build an air -defense and drone industry at scale. Second, widen 
the footprint in space, with broader constellations and stronger launch capacity. Third, invest in AI, the most under -
funded defense segment, and fuse it with the cyber shield. Automation and robotics are the multiplier. Autonomous 
mobility advances from the car industry can transfer, and R&D should be pooled across sectors. Joint investment 
with semiconductor, software and base -metal producers should lift material quality, onboard intelligence and 
computing power. Quantum belongs in the same pipeline. The primary goal is a leading-edge European model that 
can be exported, not an assembly of imported parts.  
…and lose your best trade engine and political stabilizer . For years, aerospace and aircraft have been Europe's 
most performing segment in trade exchanges, generating a consistent EUR30–35bn annual surplus toward non-EU 
members. More recently, the ramp -up in defense capacity has added EUR1–3bn per year in weapons and 
ammunition exports, a contribution that is far from negligible. But this is not just about trade balances. European 
defense expertise is widely acknowledged abroad and serves as the most effective diplomatic connector during 
high-level political engagements with international partners. It is, in essence, a powerful geopolitical tool: defense 
equipment and know -how open doors to broader bilateral agreements, strengthen alliances and help counter 
global fragmentation. Failing to inves t in military modernization does not just weaken Europe's security – it risks 
dismantling the very engine that fuels its trade influence and strategic relevance on the world stage.

Drones and space are the bright spot, AI and robotics are under-invested. Europe’s defense technology is being 
rebuilt, but so far capital transmission has mostly being channeled where Europe was the weakest and political will 
was strongest: drones collected near half of defense -related investment in the European venture capital market 
(44% since 2020) , and space and satellite imagery follow. In the meantime, AI-for-defense has attracted almost 
nothing, and robotics only a fraction of drone funding. Yet warfare is shifting toward digital and  electric-powered 
assets. The economy runs on algorithms, data centers and power grids, which makes them primary targets, as this 
year’s Middle East conflict showed .  In this context, European governments and defense primes should (1) treat AI 
as a strategic asset and open procurement to outside technology firms; (2) use co -investment and guaranteed 
demand to channel private capital into robotics and autonomy, which should also ease the sector’s labor shortages, 
and (3) protect data centers and grids as defense infrastructure. The US and China are investing heavily in both 
fields and could turn that lead into a battlefield edge. Europe should not repeat in AI the delay it has just started to 
correct in drones.

Document 297: DeepSeek全面开源推理框架：兼容英伟达、华为昇腾芯片，单集群日吞吐155万亿Token，每卡每小时收入38美元

Source PDF: all report pdf/DeepSeek全面开源推理框架：兼容英伟达、华为昇腾芯片，单集群日吞吐155万亿Token，每卡每小时收入38美元.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 4
Status: ok

### Page 1

DeepSeek 全⾯开源推理框架 DeepInfer 及 Token 编解码组件 deepseek-recipe ，兼容英伟
达与华为昇腾平台。基于该框架， DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 单集群⽇吞吐达 155 万亿 Token ，
按⾕电价计算每卡每⼩时收⼊ 38 美元。通过 Prefill 与 Decode 分离、 KVCache 复⽤及投机
解码等技术， DeepSeek 旨在提升推理效率、降低算⼒成本与能源消耗。
摘要：
DeepSeek 进⼀步开放⼤模型推理基础设施。
继开源⼤模型、 TileLang 编译⼯具及⼀系列推理算⼦库后， DeepSeek 宣布全⾯开源内部推理框
架 DeepInfer ，并同步开源 Token 编解码组件 deepseek-recipe ，旨在帮助开发者和企业优化⼤规
模模型部署，降低 AI 服务的算⼒成本与能源消耗。
DeepSeek 介绍，基于 DeepInfer ，团队已实现 DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 的⾼吞吐、低延迟⼤规模

推理服务。⽬前，单⼀集群的服务吞吐量达到每天 155 万亿 Token ，按⾕电价计算，每卡每⼩时
收⼊为 38 美元。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⻅闻历 搜索

### Page 2

推理框架是连接⼤模型能⼒与终端⽤⼾的关键基础设施。随着 AI 应⽤调⽤量增⻓，推理服务的成
本不仅取决于模型本⾝，还受到计算、通信、任务调度及缓存管理等环节的影响。如何在满⾜
延迟要求的同时提⾼有效吞吐，已成为⼤规模 AI 服务降本增效的重要环节。
兼容英伟达与华为昇腾，针对不同推理阶段优化算⼒配置
DeepInfer 集成⾼性能算⼦与深度优化的推理引擎，⽀持分布式推理、推测解码、 SWA Bounded
Replay 及结构化输出等能⼒，可在英伟达与华为昇腾平台运⾏。未来， DeepSeek 新模型的推理
代码及后续性能优化也将在该框架中持续开源。
⾯对⼤规模 AI 调⽤需求， DeepSeek 将推理系统的优化重点放在吞吐量与延迟上。系统采⽤
Prefill 与 Decode 分离部署：前者负责处理输⼊提⽰词，后者负责逐步⽣成 Token 。由于两个阶段
的计算负载不同，分开部署可以独⽴配置资源，分别优化⾸个 Token ⽣成时间和后续 Token ⽣成
间隔。

### Page 3

在硬件组织上， DeepSeek 使⽤常⻅的 8 卡机器承担 Prefill 任务，并采⽤ 64 ⾄ 128 卡超节点承担
Decode 任务。同时，系统通过⾃动扩缩容动态调整两个阶段的实例⽐例，减少流量变化导致的
资源错配；在 MoE 模型推理中，则通过专家并⾏与动态负载均衡，降低不同计算卡之间的负载
差异。
KVCache 最⻓保留 7 天，减少重复计算开销
缓存复⽤是 DeepSeek 降低推理成本的另⼀项关键设计。随着⻓上下⽂对话和 AI Agent 应⽤增
多，相同前缀可能在不同请求中反复出现。如果每次都重新处理这些内容，就会产⽣额外的计
算开销。
DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 将 KVCache 分为 Block KVCache 和 SWA KVCache 两部分，采⽤不同的存
储策略。其中， Block KVCache 存储在独⽴的 3FS 分布式⽂件系统中，相关存储容量约为 100
PiB 。根据 DeepSeek 披露的数据， Agent 流量的 KVCache 保留时间达到 3 天，聊天机器⼈流量则

达到 7 天。
当 后 续 请 求 命 中 已 有 缓 存 时 ， 系 统 可 以 复 ⽤ 此 前 的 计 算 结 果 ， 减 少 重 复 的 Prefill 计 算 。
DeepSeek 披露， 3FS 存储集群的峰值聚合读取带宽达到 10 TiB/s ，峰值聚合写⼊带宽约为 850
GiB/s ，以满⾜⼤规模缓存读写需求。
对于主要在活跃会话内短期复⽤的 SWA KVCache ， DeepSeek 则选择保留在机器内存中，不进
⾏持久化存储。线上数据显⽰，这部分缓存的保留时间约为 180 秒，命中率超过 98% 。
针对 SWA KVCache 未命中的情况， DeepSeek 采⽤ SWA Bounded Replay 机制，仅重算最近
128 个 Token ，并将注意⼒窗⼝限制在重算区间内，以降低缓存缺失带来的额外计算成本。
投机解码提升吞吐，编解码组件同步开源
除缓存复⽤外， DeepSeek 还通过投机解码和异步调度提⾼推理效率。

### Page 4

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

写评论
图⽚
DeepSeek 采⽤ DSpark 投机解码框架，根据候选 Token 的预期接受概率动态决定验证范围，避免
将验证算⼒浪费在低接受概率的候选结果上。 DeepSeek 披露，在线部署中，与固定验证 5 个
Token 的⽅式相⽐，变⻓验证使吞吐提升 6% 。
在调度环节，系统通过 CPU 与 GPU 之间的异步协作，让请求调度、前向计算和采样等操作尽可
能重叠，减少设备等待时间。此外， DeepInfer ⽀持结构化输出，可依据 JSON Schema 、正则
表达式等约束控制模型⽣成结果，满⾜特定应⽤对输出格式的要求。
DeepSeek 还同步开源⽣产级 Token 编解码组件 deepseek-recipe 。该组件⽀持 Chat Completions

API 、 Responses API 和 Messages API 等主流接⼝，并通过特殊 Token 边界识别、普通⽂本分段
并⾏编码及流式输出解析等机制，提升⻓⽂本处理效率，确保第三⽅部署的 API 处理逻辑与官⽅
服务保持⼀致。
不过， DeepSeek 并未开放推理系统的全部组件。由于 KVCache 管理和负载均衡部分与内部基础
设施耦合较深，相关代码⽬前尚未完整开源。
从模型、算⼦库到⼤规模推理框架， DeepSeek 此次进⼀步扩展了开源技术栈的覆盖范围。对于
开发者和企业⽽⾔，这有望降低构建私有化及公有云 AI 服务的技术⻔槛；对于⼤模型⾏业⽽⾔，
如何提⾼推理吞吐、复⽤计算结果并适配不同硬件平台，也正成为控制 AI 服务成本的重要⽅向。

continue, with expected growth of 1.1% in 2026 and 1.3% in 2027. In the short term, the main driver will still be exports,
particularly to Europe, which will contribute to restructuring industry. The impact of investment plans is expected to

strengthen in 2027, while domestic demand is likely to remain constrained by inflation (2.7% in 2026 and 2.6 % in 2027),
which will weigh on purchasing power. Fiscal stimulus and structural reforms are expected to continue to support growth.
However, the rise in the deficit and debt levels means that the increase in long-term interest rates observed so far is mostly
structural.
A RETURN TO GROWTH DRIVEN BY EXTERNAL TRADE
The first half of 2026 confirms the rebound in growth (0.4% and then

0.3% q/q in Q1 and Q2). This growth is driven by foreign trade (contribu-
tions from net exports of +0.8 pp and +0.2 pp, respectively, with +0.9 pp
on average coming from exports and -0.4 pp from imports), following
two years of contracting exports, particularly those to Europe (see be -
low). The increase in public expenditure ( see our analysis ) reflects
the implementation of investment plans, with the multiplier effect on
growth of these plans expected to increase.

In the second half of the year, growth is expected to remain robust,
pushing the annual average to 1.1% in 2026 (following 0.3% in 2025).
Exports are set to remain the main driver, thanks to continued strong
European demand, which is compensating for domestic demand that
remains sluggish. Growth of private consumption will, in fact, remain
subdued by energy inflation. Business investment is expected to be
driven by dynamic sectors (defence, technology equipment and aeros -

pace), which would limit the impact of rising interest rates.
In 2027, growth (1.3%) would be based more heavily on domestic de -
mand, with greater spillover effects from investment plans and a likely
recovery in household consumption (following a peak in inflation in
Q1 2027). At the same time, external demand is expected to remain
strong, but its contribution to growth would be lower than in 2026.
This is because the stronger growth in domestic demand would stimu-

late imports to a greater extent.
INDUSTRY IS BENEFITING FROM AI INVESTMENT AND INTRA-EUROPEAN
MOMENTUM
The business climate in industry improved significantly at the start of
the second half of the year (the manufacturing PMI has risen by 4 points
since May, reaching a four-year high in August). New export orders have
even reached their highest level since early 2022. These positive signs
put into perspective the risks associated with the summer’s climate

events (heatwaves and the historic drought on the Rhine), which dis -
rupted industrial activity and river transport.
Since early 2025, output in some sectors has rebounded, particularly in
the electronics sector (+6.4 %). Manufacturers of electrical equipment,
as well as IT and electronics firms, are benefiting in particular from
the wave of investment in data centres and defence. If this momentum
continues, it could offset some of the losses recorded in the automotive

and chemicals sectors, which have both seen the sharpest falls in out-
put since late 2017 (-12.8% and -19.4%, respectively).
The sustained increase in military spending on medical technologies
and robotics across Europe is driving a restructuring of the interme -
diate-goods sector. Companies exposed to struggling sectors are gra -
dually turning towards these new markets. However, this reorientation
is not fully offsetting the loss of historical markets.

The rebound in German industry is also being fuelled by European
demand. While exports to China (-12.7% YTD in July) and the United
States (-1.5%) have certainly continued to fall, exports to a number of
European Union countries have risen sharply, particularly in Southern
and Central Europe. This shift is benefiting machinery and transport
equipment (47.6% of goods exports, up 20.5% since January 2025) in
particular. We expect this momentum to continue in 2027, creating a

virtuous cycle that reinforces European growth.
A STAGNANT LABOUR MARKET
The situation in the German labour market has stabilised over the
past several quarters, but it is not yet showing signs of improvement.
The unemployment rate is shifting relatively little (4% in November;
+0.2 pp y/y) and remains below the Eurozone average (6.4%). Falling
employment in the manufacturing sector (-0.6% since Q4 2019) is offset
by an increase in the services sector (+0.9%). Nevertheless, the latest

business climate surveys suggest that the situation may stabilise. The
gradual retraining of the workforce in struggling sectors is helping to
meet some of the needs arising from new investment.
Generally speaking, in the longer term, pension reform should support
the labour supply. As a matter of fact, it will help to increase the num-
ber of workers gradually, notably through the phasing out of early reti-
rement at 63 and the indexing of the retirement age to life expectancy

from 2032 onwards.
GERMANY
Growth and inflation

The bank

world
Although the unemployment rate is set to remain low, wage growth
would remain limited, particularly in 2026, as negotiated wages will
offset inflation to a lesser extent than they will in 2027. The planned
increases in the minimum wage (+8% in 2026 and +5% in 2027, to
EUR 14.6 per hour), significant as they are, will have little knock-on

effect on the rest of the wage scale.
PURCHASING POWER CONTINUES TO BE SQUEEZED BY HIGH INFLATION
Wage purchasing power is expected to fall further in 2026, having
already lost 4 pp since 2022, before stabilising in 2027. The reduction
in income tax (by EUR 10 billion, or 0.2% of GDP), which will come into
force next January, is also expected to support the disposable income of
low- and middle-income households.
Inflation has been rising again since the start of the summer and is

expected to remain above 2% throughout our forecast horizon. It has
accelerated since June (+0.5 pp to +2.9% y/yin August) driven by a fur -
ther rise in energy prices (+6.7 pp to +9.4% y/y), while core inflation re-
mains stable at +2.6%. From spring 2027 onwards, the expected easing
in energy prices (particularly gas prices) should bring down inflation.
However, this decline will be limited by the inflationary effects of the
wider roll-out of investment plans, as well as by the expected rise in

food prices.
This inflation, which has been structurally higher since the onset of the
2022 energy shock, will weigh on household consumption. Furthermore,
German households’ propensity to save remains high by international
standards (with a net balance 15 points higher on average over the
last ten years, according to the European Commission), which is also
weighing on their consumption.
FISCAL STIMULUS CONTINUES
After recording a public deficit of -3% of GDP in 2025, we anticipate a

further deterioration in the public balance to -4% in 2026, then -4.2%
in 2027. The primary deficit is projected to reach -2.8% of GDP in 2026
(after -1.9% in 2025) before narrowing slightly to -2.7% in 2027. At the
same time, the interest burden is expected to rise gradually from 1.1%
of GDP in 2025 to 1.5% in 2027. This combination will result in a rapid
rise in the debt-to-GDP ratio, from 62.6% of GDP in 2025 to 66.1% in
2026 and then 69.7% in 2027, before approaching 85% by the end of the

decade.
The yield on the 10-year Bund rose above 3.5% on 14 September, its
highest level since 2007. In addition to the increase in issues linked to
extra-budgetary funds, inflationary pressures are also contributing to
this rise. A gradual easing of inflation next year would make a mode -
rate easing of sovereign yields possible in 2027. However, yields would
remain high, at 3.2% in Q4 2027, suggesting that the bulk of the rise is
structural.
At the same time, the government is continuing to implement its pro -

gramme of structural reforms, including measures to support business
investment, health-insurance reform, pension reform and an overhaul
of the tax system. A number of measures have come into force, par -
ticularly accelerated depreciation for business investment until 2027,
which will be followed by a reduction in corporation tax from 2028.
Administrative simplification is also set to lead to an overhaul of the
tax system from January 2027 1. The health-insurance reform has been

passed, while the pension reform is still under discussion, with expecta-
tions that it will be adopted by the end of the year.
1  The main measure will be a reduction of EUR 10 billion per year in income tax for individuals on low incomes, funded in part by an increase in taxes on high earners. The bill will
be submitted to Parliament in the autumn, before being implemented from 2027.
Article completed on 14 September 2026

► 美股 走 高，主 要 受 惠科 技 與 半導 體 表 現突
出，費城半導體指數漲勢尤其明顯 。雖然近

通膨偏高 、經濟與勞動市場保持韌性 ，因此

► 隨人工智慧運算需求持續攀升 ，谷歌、亞馬

逐步轉向多元化發展。

務，預估兩者合計將佔 2027年客製化晶片出
貨量約80%。
► 隨客製 化晶 片需 求 快速增 加 ，相 關晶 片設
計、先 進製 程及 先 進封裝 需求可 望同 步提
升，涉及上述業務發展的台股供應鏈 (註)有機
會受到市場關注。

投資焦點
► 2024年全球工廠運作的工業機器人約 470萬
台，為10年前的 3倍，主要為韓國 、中國和
日本等製造業大國將其運用於汽車 、電子和
金屬加工等產業 ，並因而維持成本和品質優
勢，為競爭力重要來源之一 。雖然美國工業
機器人的普及程度仍低於亞洲主要製造業國
家，但近年積極尋求重建工業產能 、將關鍵
供應鏈遷回美國國內以及擴大半導體和製藥
生產，有可能讓美國逐步調整工業和製造層
面的策略方向。
► 傳統工業機器人與人形機器人在外觀設計與
應用場景上有明顯差異 。工業機器人通常部
署於高度標準化的環境 ，以執行範圍相對有
限且重複性高的特定作業為主 ，而人形機器
人則被設計用於多變的環境 ，並經常需要與
人類協同工作 ，因此需結合移動能力 、感知
能力與靈巧操作能力 ，才能夠執行多樣化任
務，而非受限單一功能。
工業機器人技術相對成熟且部署速度較快全球運作中的工業機器人數量穩定成長
工業機器人技術成熟且穩定成長，為製造業強國的重要競爭力來源

投資焦點人形機器人
► 人 形機 器 人 主 要由 結 合半 導 體 與AI 模 型的
「大腦」，並由感測器 、執行器 、線材、連
接器及輕量化電池驅動的機身所組成 ，可透
過感知環境資訊 、思考並進行協調的實體動
作。投行預估至2030年，執行器將佔整體人
形機器人成本超過 50%，為價值含量最高的
核心零組件 ，麥肯錫公司甚至認為執行器與
感測器可能成為未來供應鏈中的關鍵瓶頸。
► 機器人在實體世界中執行任務時 ，任何延遲
或連線中斷都可能帶來重大風險 ，因此相較
純數位的 AI應用 ，對即時運算 (邊緣運算 )能
力的要求更高 。而近幾年邊緣運算軟硬體能
力大幅提升 ，使本地端設備得以部署數十億
參數的 AI模型 ，並將反應延遲縮短至約 0.3
秒，使機器人能夠即時感知 、決策與行動 。
此外，過去6年風險資本對機器人、感測器及
IT 硬體的每年平均投資約 100 億美元 ， 但
2026年僅上半年即已達 280億美元 ，暗示市
場對機器人產業潛力的期待有別於過往。
機器人技術進展快速並開始吸引風險資本投資人形機器人運作有賴AI模型與大量機身零組件整合

### Page 8

8
股市
◆ 美、歐、日央行升息抑通膨 ，公債殖利率走高壓抑股債市場近期動能 。然而包括油價自高檔回落使
通膨壓力稍緩 ，以及美股第三季獲利年增率仍為雙位數成長 ，股市表現有支撐 。市場持續觀察AI資
本支出是否帶來獲利成長 ，短期內評價面壓力將增添市場動盪 ，可分散配置於不同國家與產業 ，降
低市場波動影響 。總結上述，地緣衝突、殖利率偏高及評價面壓力等紛擾帶來負面影響 ，可布局防
禦股抗震，長期而言，科技及AI半導體發展趨勢不變且基本面佳，可逢拉回時分批布局。
◆ 歐、日股可作為分散配置重點 ，加上全球利率走揚有利銀行股 。此外，地緣衝突頻傳 ，歐洲國防軍
工仍有投資題材。
策略風格：短期可增加防禦股配置，中長
期看好AI題材如科技及半導體，非AI部分
可關注金融產業
區域：日本銀行股、歐洲金融、歐洲國防
債市
◆ 全球公債殖利率攀升 ，且最新聯準會利率點陣圖顯示 ，官員預估今年底前仍有一碼升息空間 ，立場
偏向維持較高利率水平抑制通膨發展 ，加上9月製造業及服務業PMI優於預期，殖利率偏高環境建議

以中短天期債券投資為主 ，可逢利率走升時布局進行鎖利領息 。高信評債體質較佳 ，具穩定現金流
與還款能力，聚焦風險調整後利差具吸引力者，包含金融、科技、通訊及公用事業等產業。
◆ 可以非美貨幣債券如歐元或澳幣計價投資級債券，分散單一貨幣風險。
債種：中短天期高信評債鎖利，產業如金
融、科技、通訊及公用事業等
衛星配置：非美貨幣債券分散風險
匯市
◆ 聯準會升息且態度偏鷹，美元高檔盤整。
◆ 日本銀行9月升息，但前瞻指引偏中性，未如市場預期的鷹派，日圓震盪。
美元高檔整理，日圓震盪
商品
◆ 中東地緣衝突加劇推升油價，短期紛擾難解，偏高的油價可能對通膨及實體經濟帶來影響。
◆ 短期受美國雙率走高有壓 ，惟美日財政赤字壓力與去美元化使全球央行買盤依舊 ，提供黃金中長期
穩定支撐。

### Page 15

個別客戶因年齡、投資能力、金融商品交易經驗及風險承受度不同，適合之服務及/或金融商品亦有不同，應進一步與專屬理財業務人員進行評估。投資人應徹底瞭解相關服務及金融商品之風險 ，並審慎評估個人財力、資產狀況、
理財需求及風險承受能力，獨立判斷並自行決定是否投資及承擔有關風險。投資任何商品皆具有風險，商品以往之績效不保證未來之投資收益，本公司不負責商品之盈虧，也不保證最低之收益。
本公司保留對本文件及其內容之著作權及其他相關權利，任何人未經本公司同意，不得翻印或作其他任何用途使用(包括但不限於傳送或轉發予其他人士等)。如有違反情事，本公司除不負任何責任外，亦將對未經同意之使用與散布
等行為究責。
基金警語
投資一定有風險，基金投資有賺有賠，申購前應詳閱公開說明書
【信託產品投資須知及風險預告：（請投資人審慎評估下列各項條文內容）】
1. 基金（包括具有定期配息之基金）投資具投資風險，此一風險可能使信託本金發生全部虧損。
2. 基金委託人須自負盈虧，本公司受託投資不保本不保息，除盡善良管理人注意義務外，投資所可能產生的本金虧損、匯率損失、或基金解散、清算、移轉、合併等風險，均由委託人承擔。

3. 本公司受託投資之基金經金管會核准或同意生效，惟不表示絕無風險。基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良管理人之注意義務外，不負責基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益，委託人
申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
4. 投資前應詳閱風險預告及各該基金公開說明書 ，確實作好財務規劃與風險評估 。本公司受託投資之基金已備有公開說明書 (或其中譯本)或投資人須知【前述文件併揭露有關基金應負擔之費用 (境外基金含分銷費用)】，委託人可
至本公司營業處所索取或至公開資訊觀測站、基金資訊觀測站或本公司網站下載。
5. 為保護既有投資人之權益，部份境外基金設有價格稀釋調整機制及公平市價規定，投資人申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書及投資人須知。
6. 委託人瞭解並同意受託人辦理特定金錢信託業務(即委託人委託投資基金)交易時，自交易相對人取得之報酬、費用、折讓等各項利益，得作為受託人收取之信託報酬；另委託人在持有基金期間 ，受託人仍持續收受經理費分成報
酬，委託人可至受託人(即本公司)網站 (https://www.kgi.com.tw/zh-tw/)查詢基金之通路報酬。其基金經理費率及其分成費率與其他通路報酬最新變動情形，亦可至該網站查詢。

7. 後收型基金在贖回時，基金公司將依持有期間長短收取不同比率之遞延申購手續費 ，該費用將自贖回總額中扣除；另後收型基金若有收取分銷費 ，則申購手續費雖可遞延收取，惟每年仍需支付0%~1.06%的分銷費（按基金日平
均淨資產價值計算），可能造成實際負擔費用增加（分銷費依各基金公司而有不同，請參考本公司後收型基金投資費用暨特約事項及各基金之投資人須知），另該費用已反映於每日基金淨值中，委託人無需額外支付。
8. 委託人之交易如經基金公司認定為短線交易者，基金公司或本公司可拒絕受理申請轉換或申購要求，並可請求支付一定比例之贖回費用或其他相關費用，費用標準均依各該基金公司之規定。
9. 委託人因不同時間進場，將有不同之投資績效，過去之績效亦不代表未來績效之保證。基金配息率不代表基金報酬率，且過去配息率不代表未來配息率；基金淨值可能因市場因素而上下波動，投資人於獲配息時，宜一併注意基
金淨值之變動。又投資新興市場可能比投資已開發國家有較大的價格波動及流動性較低的風險，委託人應慎選投資標的。
10. 部分基金可能持有衍生性商品部位，該部位可能超過基金淨資產價值之100%，並可能因而造成基金淨值高度波動及衍生其他風險。

11. 基金的配息可能由基金的收益或本金中支付。任何涉及由本金支出的部分，可能導致原始投資金額減損；且部分基金配息前未先扣除應負擔之費用。由本金支付配息之相關資料，投資人可至證券投資信託事業或總代理人之公司
網站查詢。
12. 非投資等級債券基金（本類基金主要係投資於非投資等級之高風險債券且配息來源可能為本金）（主要特色係投資前揭基金之組合基金亦同），適合能承受較高風險之『非保守型』委託人且不宜占投資組合過高的比重。由於非
投資等級債券之信用評等未達投資等級或未經信用評等，且對利率變動的敏感度甚高，可能會因利率上升、市場流動性下降，或債券發行機構違約不支付本金、利息或破產而蒙受虧損，不適合無法承擔相關風險之委託人；又如
係本國投信機構募集者，則可能投資於符合美國Rule 144A規定具有私募性質之債券，雖其投資總金額不得超過基金淨資產價值之百分之三十，然該債券具有債券發行人違約之信用風險、利率風險、流動性風險、交易對手風險
及因財務訊息揭露不完整或因價格不透明導致波動性較大之風險，投資人應審慎評估；另證券投資信託事業募集之債券型基金、平衡型基金、不動產證券化基金、以追蹤、模擬或複製標的指數表現之指數股票型基金（ETF）及

指數型基金或募集之基金投資新興市場國家債券達基金淨資產價值之百分之六十以上者，或有可能投資符合美國Rule 144A規定具有私募性質之債券，亦應留意相關風險。
13. 投資於中國之基金應遵守中華民國相關法令之規定：境外基金 (不含經金管會核准向證交所申請上市或交易之境外 ETF)如有投資大陸地區之有價證券者，以掛牌上市有價證券及銀行間債券市場為限 ，且投資前述有價證券總金額
不得超過該境外基金淨資產價值之百分之二十(境外基金機構依鼓勵境外基金深耕計畫向金管會申請並經認可者得適用鼓勵境外基金深耕計畫所列放寬前揭投資總金額上限至百分之四十之優惠措施 )；證券投資信託基金雖可投資
大陸地區之有價證券，惟仍應符合信託契約、公開說明書、證券投資信託基金管理辦法等相關規定及受到基金公司QFII額度之限制。又投資人須留意中國市場之政府政策、法令、會計稅務制度、經濟與市場等變動所可能產生之
投資風險。
14. 各基金於公開（含簡式）說明書或投資人須知揭露之風險報酬等級，係依據投信投顧公會「基金風險報酬等級分類標準」而訂定，本公司亦依據投信投顧公會「基金風險報酬等級分類標準」訂定基金產品風險等級。

富管理客服專線或透過財富管理客服信箱服務，查詢申訴案件處理進度。

Source PDF: all report pdf/Postcard from Bangkok IMF meetings_ don’t take resilience for granted (2026-10-19).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-19
Pages: 2
Status: ok

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
3
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.38 / 19 October 2026EDITORIAL
POSTCARD FROM BANGKOK IMF MEETINGS: DON’T TAKE RESILIENCE FOR GRANTED
1 See EcoNews for more details.
The global economy’s unexpected resilience might have invited the self-congratulation that is a trademark of internatio-
nal official meetings. But policymakers gathered in Bangkok for the 2026 IMF Annual Meetings were wearily aware that
“continued resilience cannot be taken for granted”, as noted in the prologue to the closing Chair’s Statement to the Inter-

national Monetary and Financial Committee meeting. Growth forecasts remain in line with those made a year ago, when
interest rates were much lower and no energy shock was in the cards, and even with those made in April, when a short
shock was the base case. But what has sustained global growth against the odds so far could falter before the headwinds
ease: oil buffers are being depleted; fiscal policy urgently needs to shift from support to deficit reduction as interest rates

remain persistently higher; and the AI boom that has been a salvatory growth engine comes with serious questions about
its sustainability and long-term economic effects. In addition to these global issues, the combination of fiscal challenges
and elections in France and the US were seen as key risks, while some topics that had until recently been in the spotlight
found themselves overshadowed.
BETTER GROWTH THAN EXPECTED FOR MOST , WORSE INFLATION FOR ALL

Despite the energy shock and surge in interest rates, the IMF's
latest global growth forecast is unchanged from a year ago and April
— at 3.1% in 2026 and 3.4% in 2027 — although the geographical
breakdown has shifted: war-exposed countries and many energy im -
porters have been downgraded, while AI-exposed countries have been
upgraded. Asia is the standout winner, expected to account for 68% of
global growth in 2026 — a feat for a region highly dependent on Gulf

energy. Compared to July, the growth forecast for advanced economies
is revised up to 2.1% in 2026 (+0.4pp, driven by Germany) and remains
at 1.8% in 2027. Among emerging markets, the IMF upgraded Asia and
downgraded other regions. Meanwhile, global headline inflation is ex-
pected to rise to 4.7% in 2026, mainly due to energy and food, before
easing to 4.1% in 2027 (revised +0.2pp)1.
AI OPTIMISM DOMINATES, BUT WITH SOME HEAVY UNANSWERED
QUESTIONS

The topic of AI dominated the Meetings. The broad consensus was that
it is transformational and offers great opportunities to all countries.
But, for now, it is producing a mixed bag of economic effects: a power-
ful boost to global demand, directly through an investment and trade
boom and indirectly through wealth effects from stock-market gains;
but also much higher interest rates (fuelled by the borrowing needed
to fund the AI build-out, and higher prices for chips, all the products

they go into, and the electricity and water data centres need).
Beyond this shared diagnosis, strong arguments could be heard on both
sides of several critical questions. The most pressing being: will AI ge-
nerate earnings commensurate with current expectations? If not — or
even if expectations are merely downgraded — expect large declines in
AI-related asset prices, a sudden stop in funding and a significant blow
to growth wherever AI has been a key engine. This is not in anyone’s

baseline for next year, but it is clearly a top risk. To what extent will AI
boost productivity over five or ten years? The IMF incorporates almost
no boost in its five-year baseline, but presents an upside scenario in
which it does. Will AI create more jobs than it destroys, raise or reduce
labour’s share of income, prove inflationary or deflationary beyond the
near term, and strengthen cyber resilience or turbocharge attacks?
And how should it be taxed to preserve the fiscal base and offset unde-

sirable labour-market effects?
A MIXED POLICY SCORECARD
Monetary policy has appropriately taken a hawkish turn nearly eve -
rywhere, and an increase in rate rises is likely in key economies, though
not enough to threaten growth. Eurozone central bankers were parti -
cularly clear in sticking to their “moderate response” playbook.
Fiscal policy, however, has yet to adjust to the end of cheap debt. With
public debt high in most countries, a growing share of national bud -

gets will be absorbed by interest payments. Yet very few countries are
consolidating. The IMF’s top call to action was to address this urgent-
ly, especially among the worst offenders — the US and France. Fiscal
restraint would also help central banks curb inflation, reducing the
need for higher interest rates.
Policies to redress global imbalances are not closer to being adop -
ted than at previous IMF Meetings, but they were not particular -
ly highlighted by the IMF this time, beyond the mantra that “the US

needs to save more, China to consume more, and Europe to invest
more”. There were reportedly good discussions behind closed doors
— at the G7 on “non-market policies and practices (NMPPs)” — and
encouraging early steps in a long journey in Europe-China talks. That
is better than nothing, but hardly enough to expect progress within a
reasonable timeframe. Meanwhile, the risks of a disorderly unwind will
keep rising.
TWO ZONES OF CONCERN IN FOCUS: FRANCE AND THE US

France was a top concern for many participants. They feared a repeat
of the 2010–2012 Eurozone debt crisis, seeing a country with public
debt approaching 120% of GDP , sub-par growth, a poor record of fiscal
consolidation, riots in the streets and presidential elections six mon -
ths away, with populist candidates leading the polls. French and Eu -
ropean policymakers, alongside private-sector participants, presented
a different picture, namely: a fiscal situation that is serious, but is

being managed (the draft budget provides for meaningful adjustment
and is very likely to be adopted without being weakened); a widening
consensus from centre-left to far-right which favours a return to fiscal
responsibility, in line with a similar shift in public opinion; and the
electoral process is only beginning, with the outcome still wide open —
not least because the candidates’ names and numbers are not known.

### Page 2

4
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.38 / 19 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
It was also pointed out that the quantum of fiscal adjustment required
is not out of line with previous consolidation episodes in France in the
2010s and the 1990s. Still, most participants left not much reassured
and expecting a bumpy ride ahead.
US participants outside government saw a growing likelihood of a De -
mocratic sweep in the midterm elections, Either way, they expected

little fiscal consolidation, despite a manifestly challenging fiscal path
that may end up causing volatility in financial markets. Non-US parti -
cipants also feared growing policy unpredictability.
OVERSHADOWED TOPICS: THE STARS OF RECENT IMF MEETINGS
Tariffs were entirely absent from the agenda and conversations. Toke-
nised finance (rather than the narrower topic of stablecoins) still fea -
tured, but far less prominently: as the field takes shape, discussion

has shifted from the conceptual what and why to the technical how.
Climate change and low-income countries remained on the agenda,
but far less in focus than at previous Meetings. The sheer number of
issues demanding attention may be overwhelming financial policyma-
kers’ bandwidth.
*****
All in all, while the global context remains extraordinarily challenging,
it’s comforting to see that global financial policy makers are broadly
alert to both risks and opportunities, and to what needs to be done for

the latter to prevail. Now they need to deliver.
Isabelle Mateos y Lago
isabelle.mateosylago@bnpparibas.com

Document 376: Romania’s inflation problem_ why disinflation is proving so difficult

Source PDF: all report pdf/Romania’s inflation problem_ why disinflation is proving so difficult.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-19
Pages: 9
Status: ok

### Page 1

Romania’s economy has cooled, but inﬂation hasn’t. Years of
wage-driven demand, repeated energy and tax shocks, and
stubborn services inﬂation are making the road back to price
stability unusually slow
Valentin Tataru
A succession of shocks has kept Romania's inﬂation ﬁght alive
Romania has not looked like an overheating economy for more
than two years. Growth has stalled, wage growth has slowed
sharply, real incomes are under pressure, and ﬁscal policy has

tightened. Yet underlying inﬂation remains stubbornly high relative
to both the rest of Europe and the National Bank of Romania’s
(NBR) target.
This disconnect between a cooling economy and persistent
inﬂation is Romania's central macroeconomic story. While the
country is still undergoing some income and price level
convergence with the euro area – making a modest inﬂation
diﬀerential structurally normal – that benign catch-up has been
overwhelmed by much stronger forces. Years in which nominal

### Page 2

incomes and domestic demand outpaced productive capacity have
been followed by a succession of overlapping energy, tax, and
administered-price shocks, each feeding into wages, services, and
short-term expectations before the previous shock had time to
fade.
The headline ﬁgure is falling, but the
underlying picture remains fragile
Romania’s annual consumer price inﬂation fell from 10.4% in June
to 6.2% in August, before edging up to 6.6% in September (6.5% on

the EU-comparable HICP measure, with prices rising 0.77% in
September alone). Yet this drop overstates the underlying
improvement. Much of the decline reﬂects favourable base eﬀects
as the sharp 2025 increases in electricity prices, VAT, and excise
duties drop out of the annual comparison.
A better gauge is the NBR’s adjusted CORE2 index, which excludes
administered prices and volatile components. Down from 8.5% at
the end of 2025 to 6.2% in September, core inﬂation remains

elevated and shows little sign of decoupling from the headline rate.
This distinction matters. One-oﬀ VAT or utility hikes automatically
drop out of annual inﬂation after 12 months, even though the
higher price level stays in place. The real question is whether such
shocks leave lasting marks on wage demands, rents, service prices,
expectations and so on. In Romania, a meaningful share of each
shock has done exactly that.
A relay race of shocks
Disinﬂation has been so slow partly because Romania has rarely

enjoyed a clean window for one shock to fade before the next
arrived. The pandemic supply shock was followed by the 2022
energy crisis. Energy price caps softened the initial blow to
households but merely postponed the adjustment; their
subsequent unwinding was followed by VAT and excise hikes in
2025, resetting the price level again. Before those eﬀects could
wash through wages and contracts, a new energy shock emerged
in 2026. The NBR’s August Inﬂation Report cited geopolitical

tensions involving Iran and low Danube water levels – which
constrained hydro and nuclear output – among the factors pushing
fuel and electricity costs higher, prompting the bank to raise its end-
2026 inﬂation forecast to an optimistic 6.1%.
Because inﬂation is path-dependent, this sequence is more than a
statistical twist. A temporary shock that leaves wage negotiations
and pricing behaviour unchanged eventually vanishes from the
inﬂation rate; a shock embedded into contracts and expectations

does not.
Romania is unusually vulnerable to this dynamic. Food and energy
make up a larger share of household spending – and energy
represents a larger share of production costs – than in Western
Europe. The same global commodity shock therefore hits Romania
harder both mathematically and psychologically. Because food,
fuel, and utility bills are the prices consumers observe most
frequently, repeated spikes encourage households and workers to
anchor their decisions to the most recent inﬂation prints rather

than the central bank's target.
Services: where the shock becomes
domestic
In September, food inﬂation stood at 3.0% and non-food goods at
7.1%, while services inﬂation reached 11.1%. Services are the
natural home of domestically generated persistence because they
are labour-intensive and largely sheltered from international
competition. A manufacturer facing higher local costs can
automate, relocate, or lose market share to imports. A restaurant,

hairdresser, dental clinic, or maintenance ﬁrm cannot import its
service from Germany; its costs and customers are local, making
wages, rent, and domestic expectations the primary drivers of
pricing.
While the 11.1% services ﬁgure also reﬂects lumpy adjustments in
rents and regulated utilities, the broader message is clear: inﬂation
has migrated from energy and traded goods into prices set inside
Romania.

### Page 4

Wages: the legacy of a boom that has
already ended
Wages are central to this story, though their timing matters more
than their current growth rate. At the macro level, unit labour-cost
growth roughly equals wage growth minus productivity growth:
Unit labour-cost growth ≈ wage growth − productivity growth
When wage growth outpaces productivity, the cost of producing a
unit of output rises, forcing ﬁrms to accept lower margins, cut
hiring, or raise prices.

Romania experienced an intense version of this gap. Over the ﬁve
years to 2024, real wages cumulatively outpaced productivity
growth by more than 20 percentage points. Fuelled by sharp
minimum-wage hikes and a nearly 20% jump in public-sector pay,
nominal unit labour costs surged by an estimated 18% in 2024
alone.
That acceleration is now over. By August 2026, average net wage
growth had slowed to 5.6% year-on-year, below inﬂation, meaning
real wages are contracting, while hiring has softened. Rather than

an active wage-price spiral, Romania is experiencing lagged wage-
cost pass-through: service ﬁrms are still adjusting prices to the
massive cumulative cost increases of 2023 and 2024. While this
suggests unit labour costs will stabilise if productivity recovers,
structural constraints, such as emigration, demographic ageing,
low participation, and skills mismatches, remain unresolved
beneath the cyclical slowdown, ready to reignite wage pressures

once demand returns.
Imports: the safety valve that shows up as
a deﬁcit
When domestic supply cannot keep pace with demand, imports
act as a safety valve. Both household consumption and capital
investment in Romania are heavily import-intensive; when the
government raises wages, pensions, or infrastructure spending, a
large share of that demand leaks abroad.

### Page 5

For several years, excess domestic demand was absorbed not just
by higher local prices, but by a widening trade gap, resulting in a
current account deﬁcit of 8.2% of GDP in 2024 and 7.9% in 2025,
the largest in the EU. Imports thus prevented domestic inﬂation
from rising even higher, but at the cost of a severe external
imbalance and greater vulnerability to global commodity prices
and exchange-rate shifts. Inﬂation and the current account deﬁcit

are ultimately two sides of the same coin: domestic demand
persistently outrunning productive capacity.
Fiscal policy: yesterday’s accelerator,
today’s brake
Romania's ﬁscal deﬁcit and ﬁscal impulse are not the same thing.
The deﬁcit widened to roughly 9.3% of GDP in 2024, supported by
rapid increases in public wages, pensions and other expenditure.
That ﬁscal expansion contributed directly to consumption, labour-
market pressure and the external deﬁcit. The deﬁcit subsequently

narrowed to around 7.7% in 2025 and is expected to decline further
in 2026 to around 6.0%. The improvement reﬂects the
consolidation packages adopted in the second half of 2025,
including higher VAT and excise rates and freezes on public sector
wages and pensions.
The inﬂation eﬀect runs in two directions. Higher indirect taxes and
administered prices initially raise the CPI. But consolidation also
reduces disposable income, consumption and companies' pricing

power. The ﬁrst-round eﬀect is inﬂationary; the subsequent
demand eﬀect is disinﬂationary.
This explains how Romania can combine weak activity with still-
high inﬂation. Romania is currently caught between those two
stages. The inﬂation data continue to reﬂect the impact of higher
taxes, energy costs and regulated price increases introduced as
part of the adjustment, even as the same ﬁscal correction is
weighing on demand. Fiscal policy is now contributing to the cure,

but some of the instruments used to achieve the cure temporarily
worsen the inﬂation print.

### Page 6

The NBR and monetary policy: constrained,
not cosmetic
Despite inﬂation sitting well above the NBR’s 2.5% target,
expectations have not fully de-anchored. Instead, anchoring has
become horizon-dependent. Professional forecasters still see
longer-term inﬂation near the top of the target band (with the NBR
projecting 3.4% by the end of 2027), showing the target still
anchors the destination. However, households and ﬁrms base their
short-term pricing and wage decisions on the supermarket prices

and utility bills they see today, leaving the target with little grip
over the near-term route.
Meanwhile, NBR has held its policy rate at 6.50%, with the lending
facility at 7.50% and the deposit facility at 5.50%, since the two 25
basis point cuts delivered in 2024. Romania's monetary
transmission mechanism can be imperfect. A persistent liquidity
surplus in the banking system can push money-market rates
toward the lower end of the central bank's corridor, reducing the

informational value of the headline policy rate. But this does not
make the policy rate irrelevant. The NBR can manage liquidity
directly, while monetary policy also operates through bank lending
conditions, government yields, asset prices, expectations and –
importantly for Romania – the exchange rate.
It is also misleading simply to subtract today's inﬂation rate from
today's policy rate and conclude that monetary policy is loose.
Investment and borrowing decisions depend on expected inﬂation

over the period during which those decisions pay oﬀ. If inﬂation is
expected to decline toward 3.0-4.0%, nominal borrowing costs
above 5.0% imply materially positive ex-ante real rates. The NBR's
constraint is therefore not simply a reluctance to tighten. With the
economy near stagnation, ﬁscal policy tightening, and supply-side
shocks unresponsive to rate hikes, the NBR is sensibly relying on
ﬁscal drag and weak consumption to do the heavy lifting – even if

the lags are long.
The exchange rate: stabilising inﬂation,
delaying adjustment
